ParlaMint-PL_2019-03-14-sejm-78-2.u6	Mr. Marshal! Mr. President! Prime Minister! Ladies and gentlemen of the Ministers! High Izbo! Dear members of the Diplomat Corps! Your Excellency! Ladies and gentlemen! For the second time, I took part in the honour of speaking before the High Chamber to present, as Minister of Foreign Affairs of the Republic of Poland, information on foreign policy to the Sejm, gathered guests and citizens of the Republic of Poland. The Exposé of the Minister for Foreign Affairs must meet two conditions: be a report on the previous achievements of the government and be an indication of plans for the future, of the defined challenges and the ways in which they will be faced. I submit to the High House and the citizens of the Republic of Poland a report on the achievements of the Government of the United Right in the international arena after the elections in 2015, including the activities of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in times where I have the honour to direct it, and during the period of responsibility of my predecessor, Mr Witold Waszczykowski. I would also like to emphasise the role of President Andrzej Duda, Prime Minister Mateusz Morawiecki and all ministers as co-creators of foreign policy. I also express my gratitude to the Sejm and the Senate of the Republic of Poland for their extremely important and effective activity in parliamentary diplomacy. The cooperation of all institutions involved in foreign policy, the combination of visions, objectives and actions of people who have the most important offices in the country has made Poland an active and listening country, which is increasingly respected by foreign partners. [[Applause]] High Izbo! Today's speech is the 30th speech of the Foreign Minister after the turn of 1980, when the Poles defeated the communist system. It was the Solidarity movement and the result of the parliamentary elections of 4 June 1989 that launched a chain of events that caused the walls to fall between the East and the West. We remember, and let us be proud of it, that it was us Poles who started our march, and basically the march of all Central and Eastern Europe, towards the desired freedom. Without victory, Solidarity would not fall the Berlin Wall and overcome the division of the continent. [[Applause]] June -89 also started the process of restoring Poland's rightful place in the world. Polish diplomacy could begin to pursue deep-rooted European and Atlantic aspirations in our society. We have come a long way from a country dependent on the Soviet Union to one of the pillars of NATO and the co-host of the European project. We would like this year's celebration of the 30th anniversary of June to be an opportunity to show Poland's place in the democratization of Europe. Parliament has a particular role to play, especially the Senate, which was elected in a fully democratic manner on 4 June 1989, and the overwhelming victory of the candidates for Solidarity has shown real social aspirations. We believe that diplomacy is a service to citizens who also actively co-create it. They do so indirectly by choosing the President from among themselves and political representation in Parliament, which sets out the Prime Minister and gives a vote of confidence to the Council of Ministers, and by participating directly in a public debate on foreign policy or, as was the case last year, in a civil consultation on the future of Europe. Mr. Marshal! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, Poland's accession to the European Union was the culmination of the efforts of all governments created after the victory of Solidarity in 1989. On 1 May we will celebrate the fifteenth anniversary of this event. Membership in the European Union has become a permanent platform for modernising our country, our economy and our foreign policy. Our citizens and our companies benefit from open borders and the common market. From the budget of the European Union, we receive funds for investments contributing to the country's competitiveness and prosperity. In 2004, our gross domestic product per person accounted for 47% the EU average, and today it is 70%. The government's objective is to match the Union's most developed countries in terms of living standards in the near future, which is conducive to 5% economic growth. 2019 is an extremely important year for the Union. The elections to the European Parliament will start a new institutional cycle in May. This creates the opportunity and the need to reflect on the priorities that we need to pursue. The next week's European Council will launch a discussion on key internal market policy objectives. On 9 May, we will have an informal meeting of Heads of State and Government in Sibiu on the future of the European Union, and in June the European Council will be devoted to the adoption of a new strategic agenda for the European Union. Polish diplomacy will be active in discussions on the future shape of the European Union. On 1 May, the 15th anniversary of the accession of Poland and other countries of the region to the European Union, at the invitation of Prime Minister Mateusz Morawiecki, leaders of the Member States that have joined the European Union since 2004 will meet in Warsaw. We want to celebrate together this important anniversary for the citizens of Central Europe and at the same time express the region's opinion on the desirable directions for further development of the European Union. We want to participate actively in the discussion in Sibiu. We are in favour of a strong Union, which is supported by its states and peoples, a Union that contributes to the growth and prosperity of its societies, a Union that supports the levelling of living standards for citizens of all Member States, a Union that fully exploits the potential of the common market and respects the four freedoms - all four freedoms: the movement of goods, capital, people, but also services. Poland will work to build citizens' confidence in the institutions of the European Union. The primary objective must be to restore the Union's full, undoubtedly legitimacy, to regain the confidence of voters in the European institutions and their ability to solve people's real problems. The reform of the EU system should be accompanied by a real democratic mandate under the national electoral process. National parliaments should play a key role, reflecting the wishes of the citizens of our community. The threats to the European project today are also coming from populist movements, both on the left and on the right. We treat these threats as a symptom of the crisis faced by the Union, not as a cause of it. The Union institutions should act on the basis of the competences set out in the Treaties and pursue the objectives which serve all members of the Union. In our view, the controversy is also raised by proposals to extend the scope of cases in which EU decisions are taken on the basis of a majority vote. Firstly, they show that there is no action to seek compromise and that unity should be the basis for the European Union's action. Many Member States believe that not all of them are given equal attention on key issues. The thought of a large part of the British people who were in favour of leaving the European Union was probably based on the belief that the decisions taken in Brussels were not affected. Secondly, the EU's voting system favours the largest Member States and, after the brexite, the weight of the largest countries will increase even more. At the time of the implementation of this system, it was not foreseen that a large Member State such as the United Kingdom would leave the Union, which would in principle affect the possibility of building coalitions in the Council. As a result, Brexit will weaken the Community's decision-making method and make it difficult for the European Commission to balance the interests of large and smaller countries. High Seym! The European Union is not only a platform for cooperation between the Member States, but also a forum for absolving their interests. The ability to pursue its interests is a measure of the maturity of the state. Taking care of Europe's future, we are also seeking Poland's rations. We are actively working to strengthen the global competitiveness of the European economy. Together with 16 other Member States, we are calling for the strengthening of the single market and European digital policy, among others. by deepening the integration of the services sector and removing barriers in the common market. We also propose building an environment that supports digital transformation, including the strong position of the European Union in the area of artificial intelligence, intelligence and the data-based economy. We will seek to implement these demands. The threat to the European Union is protectionism, which hits businesses and workers in the EU labour market. Our vision of the European Union also envisages an effective fight against tax fraud, which is a serious challenge for many Member States. Our partners appreciate the Polish government's efforts to reduce the VAT gap. Recognizing the scale and challenges of this phenomenon, we are happy to share our experience and solutions with the Member States and institutions of the European Union, including. through expert seminars. The last seminars were held in Paris and Brussels. A major challenge for the European Union and its Member States is Brexit. The negotiated exit agreement of the United Kingdom from the European Union guarantees citizens' rights, British contributions to the budget of the European Union and provides certainty to businesses in the form of a transitional period. Thanks to the active attitude of Poland, the European Union has adopted solutions allowing Polish road and air guides to access the British market. In the case of brexitu without agreement, the British Government presented a plan to protect the citizens of the European Union, including Poles, while the Polish government adopted a package of legislative solutions protecting the rights of the British people living in our country. However, we are in the position that brexit without a contract is the worst case scenario, so we will be looking for solutions that will allow us to gain acceptance for the negotiated agreement. The shape of the future EU budget will be determined in a new political agreement in the European Parliament and in the new composition of the European Commission. We are opposed to a reduction in the EU budget and to maintaining a high level of funding for cohesion policy and the common agricultural policy. We would like to remind you that the transfer of EU funds is compensation for opening up weaker economies to competition in stronger economies, from which the latter have serious benefits. The lack of resources after the brexite should be compensated by the abandonment of the budget discounts and the increase in the contributions of the other Member States. Poland declares its willingness to increase our contribution accordingly. The Commission's proposal to make the payment of funds from the budget of the Communities conditional on the state of the rule of law currently under review in the countries to which they are allocated is unacceptable. The payment of these funds is a treaty commitment. The Commission's proposal strikes a lack of precision in the criteria for assessing the rule of law, a strongly reduced threshold for blocking decisions and, above all, a lack of legal basis for such action. Member States have not given the European Commission or the strict body to the political body, the Council, the right to judge, but only the power to refer cases to the Court of Justice of the European Union. It should also be stressed that our reservations are largely shared by the Council's lawyers who are watching over the Brussels legislative process. This position was reflected in the legal opinion of the Council Legal Service of 25 October. Mr. Marshal! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, Today's world faces unprecedented challenges that make it more unstable and unpredictable than in the past decades. The order of the world based on the prevailing belief that democracy is the best form of governance, human rights are universal and natural, and international law should form the basis of international relations, it is being systematically eradicated. The use of military force has once again become a tool for changing national borders. In this situation, the strong and consistently modernised Armed Forces of the Republic of Poland are an essential instrument for defending our borders and building our allied credibility, while diplomacy is designed to develop the most advantageous international architecture for Polish security. This month we celebrate the 20th anniversary of Poland's NATO membership. Pulling the Polish flag to the mast at the headquarters of the Alliance symbolized Poland's return to its rightful place in Europe and opened a new era with regard to our security. The strengthening of NATO's military presence on the eastern flank of the North Atlantic Alliance, including particularly the strengthening of military cooperation between Poland and the United States, remains our priority. We cooperate in this respect with a group of nine countries of our region in the Bucharest format. The final example of the joint coordinated vote of the countries of the region on risk assessment and strengthening the security of the eastern flank is the fourth. already meeting the heads of state of the Bucharest format on 28 February this year. in Košice. We particularly want to strengthen the capabilities of the Alliance's deterrent and defence. Our quest to become the core of NATO and US military presence in our region is real. NATO troops, American soldiers, but also British, Romanian and Croatian troops are stationed in our country. In Elbląg, a multinational command was established to coordinate the Alliance's operations on its eastern flank. The American armored crew is stationed in a rotating and continuous manner in western Poland. Another year is underway, coordinated by the Ministry of National Defence, work on the creation of an anti-missile base in Redzików as a permanent base of U.S. troops of strategic importance. The project of strengthening the military presence of the United States in Poland is taking shape in the ongoing bilateral negotiations. Increasing military mobility remains an important task. Mass Russian maneuvers at the eastern borders of NATO require rapid formation of allied troops that can ensure balance of forces in the region. This also requires the credibility of military reprisals. Poland has already made a significant reduction in the procedures for granting military transit permits and expects the same from allies. We are in favour of creating within NATO, to say the least, military Schengen. We support NATO's cooperation - the European Union on this issue. Like other members of the North Atlantic Alliance, we believe that the United States' decision to increase its obligations under the Treaty on the Total Elimination of Missiles and Indirect Missiles and the Medium Range, known as the INF Treaty, is a reasonable response to Russia's long-term violation. Poland is in favour of the North Atlantic Alliance maintaining open door policy. The recent signature of the Protocol on the accession of the Republic of North Macedonia is evidence of NATO's credibility in this area. We want to deepen cooperation with NATO partner countries, especially from our immediate environment: Finland, Sweden, Ukraine, Moldova and Georgia, with which we have a far-reaching convergence of assessment of the nature of the threat to international security. We seek the support of the Alliance for our eastern neighbours. The Polish-Lithuanian-Ukrainian brigade is stationed in Lublin. In February this year. It was visited by the presidents of three countries. Together with our allies, we are implementing proposals for the development of NATO-Ukraine Platform. combating hybrid threats, which also aim to strengthen resilience in areas such as critical infrastructure protection, cyber-defense or the fight against terrorism. Last year, from this stand at the NATO Parliamentary Assembly, I stressed the need to meet the commitments concerning expenditure on attendance, in accordance with the Natov criteria. Poland already spends 2% on defence GDP. We plan to gradually raise this expenditure to 2.5% in 2030, perhaps earlier. Our efforts in this area are recognised by NATO authorities and allies, as I had the opportunity to see during recent visits to Poland by NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg and US Secretary of State Michael Pompeo. Poland is placed different by these leaders, as well as others, for example to follow. At the same time, we are pragmatic about the development of defence cooperation in the European Union. We want to support the interoperability and compatibility of the armed forces of the European countries and increase the mobility of the army and military equipment. We believe that the European Defence Fund is an opportunity for the Polish arms industry to stimulate innovation and consolidate the presence of the home defence sector in the European R &amp; D cooperation chain. However, the construction of the so-called. The European Union's strategic autonomy cannot, in our view, be at the expense of NATO and the weakening of transatlantic ties. Poland is ready to share responsibility for peace and security both in Europe and beyond in the spirit of 360 degree approach. In order to defend the territory of the North Atlantic Alliance, Polish troops are present within NATO forces in Latvia and Romania, and Polish aircraft patrol the airspace of Lithuania. Poland also helps to combat terrorism, to address the causes of migration outside NATO through actions in Eastern Europe and the Balkans, Iraq and Afghanistan, the Mediterranean and Africa. Due to geographical proximity, training activities of the European Union mission in Georgia, Moldova and Ukraine are essential for us. Since the 1990s Poland has been present in NATO and European Union missions in Kosovo and Bosnia and Herzegovina. We are also engaged in the military activities of the Global Coalition to fight against the so-called. Islamic State and NATO training missions in Iraq, Jordan, Kuwait and Qatar. In Afghanistan, the Polish Armed Forces help to build local security structures. We are also returning to UN peacekeeping operations. In November this year we expect to deploy the Polish contingent in UN UNIFIL operation in Lebanon. We are participating in the EU Operation Sophia in the Mediterranean. Single Polish civilian experts, observers and military staff also work on EU missions in Libya, Mali, Central African Republic, Somalia and off the coast of the Horn of Africa. In doing so, Poland supports the strengthening of the capacity of the Sahel States to combat organised crime and terrorism and to protect borders, including by setting up joint branches, so-called. Joint forces G9 Sahel. To this end, we have provided support in the form of ammunition for the armed forces of the Republic of Chad worth 600 000. Euros. We have also offered G5 Sahel countries training in the fight against improvised explosives. In total, about 1500 Polish soldiers are currently stationed outside the country. I give them from this place my thanks for their difficult service, which is an example of solidarity with our allies, which contributes to maintaining world peace and enhancing the security of our country. [[Applause]] One of the important foreign policy objectives is to ensure energy security, which can only happen by becoming independent of Russian gas supply. We consistently oppose the implementation of the project Nord Stream 2 and we support the European Commission's critical position on this issue. The purpose of the revision of the so-called The gas directive is to include gas pipelines from third countries under European Union law. In 2018, we built a coalition of dozens of countries, which resulted in agreement on the text of the revision of the Directive, which will make it difficult for Gazprom to use its privileged position on the European market. This does not change our negative assessment of Nord Stream 2. A key project for security of supply in the region is the construction of the Baltic Pipe pipeline linking Poland through Denmark, followed by Norway with gas deposits in the North Sea. In December 2018, a bilateral agreement regulating the legal framework for the operation of the pipeline was signed. After completion in October 2022, Baltic Pipe together with LNG Terminal im. President Lech Kaczyński in Świnoujście will provide Poland with real energy independence and will also support the countries of our region. High Izbo! Poland has consistently strengthened its strategic partnership with the United States, a key ally in NATO. We have successfully developed a high-level political dialogue over the last period, which has been illustrated, among others. Visit of US President Donald Trump in Warsaw in July 2017, visit of the President of the Republic of Poland Andrzej Duda in Washington in September 2018 and a number of other meetings, including the visit of Vice President Michael Pence and Secretary of State Michael Pompeo in Poland in February this year. The prospects for the development of Polish-American cooperation are outlined in September ub. r. by the presidents of the Republic of Poland and the United States of America the declaration of the protection of freedom and the building of prosperity through the Polish-American strategic partnership. It extends the possibilities of supporting the US administration to deliver our security priorities, including strengthening the US military presence in Poland, cooperation in the energy sector and deepening trade and mutual investment. This will be served by the planned economic visits to the United States even this year, both the President and the Prime Minister of the Republic. Our priorities in relations with the US include extending the presence of US forces in Poland as part of strengthening the Eastern NATO flank, joint projects in the field of security and defence, such as the installation of the US missile defence system in Redzików and cooperation between the air forces of both countries, and the systematic expansion of cooperation both in the area of energy security and in the economic, investment and human interaction. Last year, we launched a strategic energy dialogue with the US to improve the diversification of raw materials supply. New perspectives for the Polish research and innovation sector are opened last year by the Ministry of Science and Higher Education agreement between Poland and the United States on scientific and technical cooperation. This agreement facilitates closer cooperation between scientists of both countries and Polish and American government agencies financing research. Poland's strategic partnership with the United States is a significant part of the transatlantic community. We are convinced that maintaining strong transatlantic ties is in everyone's interest on both sides of the Atlantic. Poland is a strong advocate of dialogue in the context of the different positions of the transatlantic community partners arising from different circumstances, assessments or experiences. In this context also cooperation between Poland and Canada has recently been systematically strengthened in both political and economic relations. Traditionally, security remains at the heart of our dialogue. Polish and Canadian soldiers successfully cooperate in the battalion battle group NATO in Latvia. High Seym! Mr. Marshal! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, Our most important neighbour and partner in the European Union is the Federal Republic of Germany. [[Voice from the room: Ooh!]] Germany is the dominant partner in trade for Poland, taking about 28% Polish export. The strong and stable German economy is therefore an essential prerequisite for the economic success of our country and, more broadly, of our region. The total turnover of the Visegrad Group countries with Germany is higher than in the case of similar statistics on the United States, Russia and China respectively. We are therefore a significant market for Germany, which maintains a significant number of jobs in the German economy and, at the same time, a valuable source of labour emigration, which makes a significant contribution to GDP growth behind the Oder. Last year was marked by a lively dialogue with the Germans. There have been meetings of the presidents of both countries and intergovernmental consultations with Heads of Government. My German friend and I have met five times. In a joint statement of 2 November 2018, we set out a framework for cooperation between our countries. Germany is an important country of the European Union and their decisions are crucial to the direction of reform of this organisation. In many areas we have a convergent position, among others. we are in favour of fully respecting the four freedoms in the common market, including the deepening of its digital dimension. In the debate on the future of the Union, we hope for further intensive dialogue. We note with satisfaction that Germany's efforts to transform the euro area show that they do not contribute to increasing the division within the European Union. We also welcome Germany's attitude towards maintaining sanctions imposed on Russia in connection with its aggression in Crimea and Donbass. However, we have different approaches to the project Nord Stream II, which we believe is not intended to ensure the energy security of the European Union. Other challenges in Polish-German relations remain issues related to the rights of Polish and Polish people living in Germany. The positive signal is that this year there will be a meeting, a Polish-German round table dedicated, among others. the situation of the Polish community in Germany. We also see the need to discuss historical issues, which will be promoted by the 80th anniversary of the outbreak of World War II. The issue of reparations for World War II also remains an important topic for discussion between our countries. [[Applause]] France is an important reference point for us in European, political and military and economic matters. The historical picture of friendly France is part of the Polish collective memory still from the era of partitions and the struggle to regain independence. The image of France as a swordsman of the freedom of peoples against absolute monarchies is deeply rooted in Polish culture. In our relationship, we have a great deal in common and we can achieve new goals together. For example, this year we will celebrate the Polish-French year of science, which we hope will contribute to further deepening cooperation between the societies of both countries. However, our visions of the future of the European Union are at present divergent at many points. Our doubts arise from the concept of a multi-speed Europe, protectionist action or the application of double standards to the Member States of the European Union. On the other hand, we welcome France's participation in strengthening the eastern NATO flank, e.g. in Estonia and since last year also in Lithuania. Given the importance of France in Europe and the upcoming elections to the European Parliament, developments in this country will have a significant impact on the future of the European Union, which of course is of vital interest to Poland. In addition to developing bilateral relations with France and Germany, we also want to renew cooperation with both countries within the Weimar Triangle. Our relations with Great Britain are very intense and multifaceted. These are due to close cooperation in the field of security, a strong pro-Atlanticism common to both our countries and a realistic assessment of Russia's policies. The United Kingdom has been one of the three key trading partners of Poland for years and maintaining this state is our goal. We have established various formats of cooperation, which will be maintained after the United Kingdom's withdrawal from the European Union, such as: annual intergovernmental consultations, the last one took place in December. in London, and dialogue in the square of foreign ministers and defence ministers. In turn the Polish-British Belvedere Forum, the third edition of which took place last year, contributes to a better understanding of Polish and British political, non-governmental and expert circles. We have a vision with Italy of the place of sovereign Member States in the future reformed European Union and a firm will to defend the Union's external borders against illegal immigration. This Decision shall enter Italy was Poland's fifth largest trading partner, while the trade in goods is characterised by a negative trade balance. In January Poland was visited by Italian Deputy Prime Minister Matteo Salvini, while Italian Foreign Minister took part in the Middle East conference in Warsaw. Spain and I share a far-reaching convergence of positions with regard to the European Union in terms of two speeds, an ambitious budget and the shape of the common agricultural policy and cohesion policy. Next week a visit of the Spanish Foreign Minister is planned in Warsaw Josep Borrell. We are also counting on the resumption of Polish-Spanish intergovernmental consultations. With the Netherlands, we continue constructive cooperation within the Utrecht Conference, an important forum for discussion on bilateral and EU issues. We have also been in intensive dialogue with Belgium since January this year. also within the framework of the UN Security Council. In both of these countries, President Andrzej Duda will pay a visit to celebrate the 75th anniversary of the liberation of these areas by the Polish army. The relations between Poland and the Holy See are special. This week's visit of the Secretary of State of the Holy See connected with the 100th anniversary of the restoration of diplomatic relations between the Holy See and Poland and the 6th anniversary of the pontificate of His Holiness Pope Francis provides an opportunity to summarize the common achievements and to set new directions for cooperation in the future. Mr. Marshal! Mr Fellowships and Mr Fellow Members, The development of regional cooperation in Central and Eastern Europe is an important component of the European integration process. The Community of historical experiences, cultural proximity, similar paths of socio-economic development or similar priorities in the European Union are factors that bring the countries of our region closer together. Working together, speaking in a common voice, we're just stronger. The most important regional cooperation forums are the Visegrad Group, the Tri-marriage Initiative and the Bucharest Nine. The Visegrad Group is an important framework for Poland to conduct regional policy and to realise the interests of the Central European region on the international stage. The EU agenda remains broadly understood as the core of V4 political cooperation. In January and June Ub. We have reached a common position on the future of the European Union as set out in the declarations adopted by the Prime Ministers. Our position on migration has been reflected in the decisions of the European Union as a whole. At the same time, we remain open to pragmatic cooperation in V4+ format, in particular with European neighbours. We have reactivated a group of cohesion policy's friends in which we work towards the best possible shape of the multiannual financial framework. The November Summit of the Cohesion Group in Bratislava adopted a joint declaration by 14 countries on the maintenance of existing priorities, i.e. cohesion policy and the common agricultural policy. The Trimar Initiative, a political cooperation mechanism between 12 Central European countries of the European Union in the transport, energy and the digital agenda, launched by the presidents of Poland and Croatia in 2015. We hope that the development of transport and communication links in the Northwest and South will contribute to the deepening of regional cooperation in this part of Europe and the strengthening of the European Union as a whole. The Warsaw Tri-Morza Summit in 2017 provided support to the United States, while the Bucharest Summit last year supported the European institutions and Germany, and also launched the project from the political and conceptual stage to intergovernmental budgetary decisions. The establishment of the Tri-marine Fund, the network of chambers of commerce and industry and the business forum has been initiated. We hope that along with the fourth summit already planned for June this year. In Slovenia, the initiative will gain even greater momentum. Our bilateral relations with the Czech Republic, Slovakia and Hungary are developing dynamically. Close political cooperation at the level of presidents, prime ministers and foreign ministers is complemented by rich business and civil society contacts. Growth in economic turnover and trade are a source of optimism. We will make efforts to keep this positive trend for all of us. Our cooperation with Romania is developing very well. Last year we launched a bilateral intergovernmental consultation, which we will continue this year too. The confirmation of friendly, very good relations with Bulgaria was the February visit of the Minister of Foreign Affairs of this country in Warsaw. We are also deepening the dialogue with the Baltic States. In 2018, President Andrzej Duda visited Lithuania, Latvia and Estonia, while Prime Minister Mateusz Morawiecki met with his counterparts from these countries twice. With the Baltic countries, we share the assessment of the risks arising from the East. We aim to strengthen transatlantic links and synchronise energy infrastructure. The conditions for the operation of Orlen's investment in Lithuania have also improved. Many expressions of mutual sympathy were accompanied by celebrations of the 100th anniversary of independence of Poland, Lithuania, Latvia and Estonia. In June Ub. The presidents of these countries took part in anniversary celebrations in Warsaw. We also work intensively with Denmark, Sweden, Norway and Finland. Denmark and I have signed an agreement on the delimitation of the sea border and the construction of the Baltic Pipe pipeline, which will enable the transmission of gas from Norwegian deposits. We will work with Sweden to develop the Eastern Partnership on the 10th anniversary of its creation. In two weeks' time, I will be hosting a Swedish Foreign Minister in Warsaw, with whom we will be taking part in a meeting with the Heads of Delegation of the European Union from the region, to begin reflection on the future of this initiative, the Eastern Partnership. Prime Minister Mateusz Morawiecki, in turn, will host 10 in June this year. The European Union Strategy Forum for the Baltic Sea Region, to be held - at the level of the prime ministers of these countries - in Gdansk. Poland actively supports the European integration of the Western Balkans. Last year, I visited Northern Macedonia, Albania and Serbia, taking part in the opening of the conference: Kopijski, Tiranska and Belgrad, which aims to pass on Polish experiences from the accession process to the European Union. In May this year. President Andrzej Duda will meet with the presidents of the Western Balkan countries at a conference in Tirana. This year we are chairing the Berlin Process, an initiative aimed at supporting Balkan countries in their efforts to join the European Union. The meeting of Foreign Ministers of the Berlin Process will take place in April this year. in Warsaw, a forum of cities and regions with the participation of development ministers - in June this year in Rzeszów, while the summit of the Western Balkans with the participation of several Heads of Government, Foreign Ministers, Home Affairs and Economy Ministers and representatives of business and civil society - in early July in Poznań. This will be another opportunity to confirm our commitment to stability in Europe and to establish new business contacts by Polish companies. Ladies and gentlemen! The situation in the East is a big challenge for Poland. Russia continues its aggressive policy towards Ukraine, the last sign of which is the conflict in the Kerch Strait area. This is accompanied by an intense information-propagandation war, attempts to destabilise the situation in the Balkans, the maintenance of frozen conflicts in the Caucasus and Transnistria, and acts of terror carried out in the territory of NATO and the European Union Member States. Poland is in favour of a strong response to these actions. Any dialogue with the Russian Federation within the framework of the Euro-Atlantic and European structures should be subject to compliance with the demands of the international community. In particular, we call for the release of Ukrainian seafarers imprisoned by Russia, the return of Ukrainian ships and the restoration of full freedom of navigation between the Azov Sea and the right part of the Black Sea. We also call for the implementation of the resolution of the Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe, adopted also thanks to the involvement of Polish parliamentarians, which calls on Russia to hand over to Poland the wreckage of the government aircraft TU-154. [[Applause]] In Polish-Russian relations we are also looking for areas where joint engagement can help to rebuild the conditions of cooperation. Last year, Polish and Russian experts prepared teaching aids for history teachers. An agreement was reached on the number of transport permits. We've started important talks about diplomatic property. We organized a study visit for representatives of the Russian media. The regions of north-eastern Poland and the Kaliningrad region can count on funding under a cross-border cooperation programme worth almost EUR 60 million. Poland continues to support the territorial integrity and sovereignty of Ukraine throughout the country within its internationally recognised borders. [[Applause]] Minister of Foreign Affairs of Ukraine Pawlo Klimkina accepted as part of an official visit to Warsaw in October ub.r. I was the first foreign minister to visit Kiev after the incident in the Kerch Strait. We also recall the humanitarian situation in Crimea and Donbas in the UN by holding open UN Security Council meetings. The state of Polish-Ukrainian relations in practical cooperation is good, and in the field of military cooperation, security policy and energy - even very good. Trade between the two countries increased by 9% in 2018 compared to 2017. Many Ukrainians work in Poland. Our consuls spent nearly 850 thousand last year. visas. Nearly 40 thousand are also educated in Poland. Ukrainian students. We still need to work on the historical dialogue with Ukraine. The condition of the Polish party is to withdraw the ban on the exhumation of the remains of the Polish population, which is incomprehensible to us in the light of civilization standards. We are counting on the right decisions of the Ukrainian party in this regard. The existence of independent Belarus is recognised as the basis for international governance in Central and Eastern Europe, or more widely in Europe. In the current circumstances, our bilateral relations focus on practical neighbourly cooperation. As part of the readiness presented by the Belarusian partners last year, an interministerial agreement was signed on the maintenance of road bridges at the Polish-Belarusian border and a tourist visa-free movement was introduced in the Augustów Canal area, Brest and Grodna and at the Minsk airport. We hope that the Belarusian authorities will return to the issue of a small border traffic agreement which would positively open border regions to even more intensive contacts between local communities. On a regional level, Poland is the leader in the implementation of the European Neighbourhood Instrument programmes. According to the budget criterion, the Polish-Belarus-Ukraine programme 2014-2020 is the largest cross-border programme in the European Union. Poland continues to support the territorial integrity and European and Atlantic ambitions of Georgia. Last year and at the beginning of this year, President Andrzej Duda and the Marshals of the Sejm and Senate, Marek Kuchciński and Stanisław Karczewski visited Tbilisi. The Parliamentary Assembly of the Republic of Poland and Georgia was also established. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs organized a joint visit of foreign ministers of Poland, Lithuania, Latvia and the Deputy Prime Minister of Ukraine in Georgia on the 10th anniversary of the historic visit of President Lech Kaczyński and the leaders of other countries in the region. It is also worth mentioning that since 1 January this year the Polish embassy in Tbilisi serves as NATO contact point. For Poland, the European and Euro-Atlantic integration of Georgia and other countries of the region, which have such ambitions, guarantees long-term stability and economic, political and social development of our partners. In the first half of the year we expect the President of Georgia to visit Warsaw. We support Moldova's pro-European orientation. We declare support for the European Union's greater involvement in political dialogue with Chisinau, leading to the resolution of the frozen conflict in Transnistria. We are also working on further development of dialogue with Azerbaijan and Armenia, carefully observing the political developments in these countries. In addition, Poland has consistently developed contacts with the countries of the South Caucasus and Central Asia, while respecting the principle of respect for territorial integrity and sovereign equality of states and freedom of choice for political and social development. With our partners in this region, we want to build good political relations and develop economic cooperation, among others. by participating in the construction of transport corridors linking the West to the East and expanding the economic presence of our companies in these countries. This will also be served by the planned visit of President Andrzej Duda to Azerbaijan. An effective Eastern policy implemented within the European Union is our strategic objective. As part of this policy, Poland focuses on maintaining a firm and uniform position towards Russia and as ambitious as possible agenda in relations with the Eastern Partnership countries. In 2019 we will engage in the celebration of the 10th anniversary of the Eastern Partnership. We want this year to be the time for your partner's intensive implementation of the commitments made in association agreements and the time for reflection on the future of the Eastern Partnership. We will show the citizens of the European Union that strengthening relations with the East is an investment in the stable future of the whole united Europe. Mr. Messengers! Gentlemen! Asia and the Pacific are areas of great opportunity, but also challenges for our diplomacy. Polish policy towards the region and our growing presence in it serve the economic development of the country by acquiring new markets and investments. We highly value Poland's strategic partnerships with Japan, the Republic of Korea and the People's Republic of China. This year there are round anniversaries of Poland's establishment of diplomatic relations with these countries. The celebrations of the 100th anniversary of establishing relations with Japan were inaugurated last year during the visit of the Japanese Minister of Foreign Affairs in Warsaw. This visit was also an opportunity to strengthen economic relations on trade and investment. Japan remains a key Asian investor in Poland. We appreciate the presence of Japanese companies on the Polish market for almost 30 years. Thanks to the entry into force of 1 February this year. The economic partnership between the European Union and Japan has opened up new investment and trade opportunities, including for Polish entrepreneurs. Traditionally, we attach great importance to relations with the Republic of Korea, the largest Asian investor in Poland. The direct investment of this country in our country exceeded 1 billion dollars. The Middle East conference in Warsaw was also an opportunity to conduct bilateral talks with the Korean Minister of Foreign Affairs. We are looking forward to discussions aimed at stabilising the situation in the Korean Peninsula, among others. as a member of the Supervisory Committee of Neutral States. One of our most important partners on the Asian continent remains the People's Republic of China. Trade is a key element of cooperation between Poland and the country. We hope that the activity of Polish economic diplomacy and the activities of the Polish Investment and Trade Agency, which opened its second representative office in China in Chengdu in November 2018, will contribute to improving trade balance in bilateral relations. Development of economic cooperation is also a priority in relations with India. Poland is the largest recipient of Indian investments in Central and Eastern Europe and one of the largest in the European Union. We also work with Southeast Asian countries grouped in ASEAN. Last November, I paid a visit to Indonesia, which is now, since January of this year, a permanent member - together with Poland - the UN Security Council. ASEAN countries express interest in Polish technologies, among others. in the field of agriculture. The first official visit of the President of the Republic to Australia and New Zealand in August last year was connected with the Polish-Australian Economic Forum in Sidney and business talks in Oakland. It is worth mentioning that in 2018. Polish Airlines LOT opened a direct cruise connection on the Warsaw - Singapore route. This year LOT is also expected to launch flights to South Asia, including Delhi. This is conducive to the development of business contacts and increased tourist traffic. Mr. Marshal! High Seym! The Middle East and North Africa have been an inflammatory region on the map of the world for many years, a source of threats to international terrorism and uncontrolled migration. However, this area should not only be seen in the context of security challenges but also in the context of potential opportunities, especially in terms of economic cooperation. Since the 2015 parliamentary elections Poland has dynamicised relations with countries in the Middle East and North Africa. High-level visits to Lebanon, Jordan, Israel, Libya, Sudan and the Maghreb have taken place for many years. As a non-permanent member of the Security Council We are actively working to ensure world peace. Our commitment in this area was expressed by a conference on the possibilities of security and stability in the Middle East organised jointly with the United States in February this year in Warsaw. For the first time in many years, the meeting was attended by the Foreign Ministers of the major Arab countries and the Prime Minister of Israel. At the conference we initiated the so-called. Warsaw trial. We hope that this will be the beginning of international dialogue at expert level. [[Voice in the room: You can't see it yet.]] The working groups set up in this process will be able to discuss issues such as counter terrorism, ballistic missile proliferation, maritime and aviation security, energy security, cybersecurity, humanitarian issues and refugee and human rights issues. We also support the efforts of the United States and the international community to resume the peace process in the Middle East. We are in favour of solutions to the Israeli-Palestinian conflict based on the two-state model of an independent and democratic Palestine, while guaranteeing Israel's security interests. As regards relations with Israel, we believe that the signing of the Joint Declaration of Prime Ministers in June 2018 closes last year's dispute about the amendment of the IPN Act. In this context, with sadness, we note with confusion that some Israeli politicians still face unfair accusations against Poland and Poles. Unfortunately, this is also the result of many years of neglect in the field of historical policy and the lack of adequate responses to slandering the image of our country in the world, which led to the strengthening of false stereotypes. The adoption by Polish state institutions and diplomacy of energetic steps, energetic actions in the field of dissemination of knowledge about Poland's history already brings the first tangible results. We are pleased that in the face of these accusations our position was supported by influential Jewish environments and high representatives of American diplomacy, and the American and Israeli media presented the problem of Polish-Jewish relations during World War II in a more balanced way, much more balanced than last year. With regard to the future of Polish-Israeli relations, I would like to stress at the same time that we are ready to continue our cooperation with Israel, including discussions on difficult topics related to our common history. However, we do not accept statements that duplicate false stereotypes, using a simplified vision of the fate of both nations. Our dialogue should be based on openness, mutual understanding and respect for the right of the other party. [[Applause]] High Seym! Poland is actively involved in stabilization of so-called. the southern neighbourhood of the European Union. In relations with Latin America, the challenge of the last three years has been to create favourable conditions for a comprehensive development of cooperation, particularly in the economic sphere. Since November 2015, since the United Right, Poland has signed 215 bilateral agreements with more than 60 countries and 14 multilateral agreements. At the same time 223 bilateral agreements concluded with more than 70 countries entered into force. Poland has also become a party to 50 multilateral agreements. Noteworthy is the signed November 19th Ub. r. Polish-Danish delimitation agreement located south of Bornholm so-called. The gray area. The aim of the agreement is to put an end to more than 40 years of dispute and ensure legal certainty in the Baltic Sea region. As a result of the negotiations, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs achieved significantly better results than in the case of similar solutions from other countries in the region. In agreement with the Ministry of Entrepreneurship and Technology, we conclude investment protection agreements, so-called. BIT agreements which have been declared incompatible with Union law by the Court of Justice of the European Union. A noticeable trend is also the decrease in the number of complaints against Poland to the European Court of Human Rights. In 2018, 1941 new complaints were submitted, 120 less than the year before. In comparison, 5768 new complaints were brought before the Court in Strasbourg in 2010. As a result of these trends, the number of new complaints against Poland in relation to the number of inhabitants remained below the European average last year. At the same time, we take care to enforce the judgments of the European Court of Human Rights. At the end of last year, a record low level of 100 judgments suspended in the enforcement procedure was reached. In comparison, 313 decisions were not executed in 2015 and 924 in 2011. We will be actively involved in the celebration of the 70th anniversary of the Council of Europe. The General Secretary of this organization Thorbjörn Jagland will pay a visit to Warsaw next week. The public is concerned about the JUST Act and the post-war restitution of property. It should be made clear that this law, as part of the internal American legislation, does not give rise to any obligations for other countries, in particular it does not constitute a legal basis for making legal claims on property in Poland which was taken away by Nazi Germany or by Soviet Russia during World War II and by the communist authorities after it was completed. Potential claims of entitled persons, irrespective of their nationality, shall be dealt with in court or administrative proceedings in accordance with the rules and procedures provided for in the Polish legal system on general principles. High Seym! Another important goal of diplomacy is to support the economic development of Poland. The reason for its satisfaction is that according to the World Bank ranking Poland is currently the 23rd largest economy in the world, and our above average economic growth can result in a promotion of up to two positions in the next edition of the compilation. Through an extensive network of facilities, we provide our companies with information on the specificities of individual markets and their legal and financial conditions. In 2018, our embassies and consulates provided information or intervened in favour of 4,000. Polish companies, mostly in Europe, where 80% are located Polish export. There is also increasing activity in other regions, in Asia. More than 500 companies, 490 in the Commonwealth of Independent States, 480 in the African and Middle East, and 340 in the Americas, benefited from our help. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs also offers permanent support for the Polish defence industry. We encourage companies in this sector to participate in multinational research projects, development programmes and initiatives to acquire military capabilities using the European Defence Fund. The Polish Investment and Trade Agency opens up other foreign trade offices. By the end of this year, the number will reach 70, 22 of which will be in Asia, 13 in America, 9 in Africa, remaining in Europe. A significant part of these offices have already achieved full operational capacity. We also strive to increase the participation of Polish companies in tenders and projects of international organizations. In June 2019 we will organize the first International Trade Fair for Humanitarian Aid and Development in Central and Eastern Europe Warsaw Humanitarian Expo. We would like them to be an opportunity to present Polish products and services used in humanitarian activities and development projects, ranging from food through medical equipment to IT services and renewable energy sources. The Warsaw Humanitarian Fair will also be a platform for discussion on the relations of the Polish community of NGOs with business in order to develop a better synergy of activities. In turn, the initiative to set up a Polish technology hub is aimed mainly at Asian and Middle East markets in response to the challenges faced by technologically advanced companies, especially IT companies. We will continue our involvement in the work of the Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development, supporting its extension to include further European countries and activities in Asian and African countries with which Poland is developing economic cooperation. This year's 120th anniversary of the birth of the most outstanding Polish economist Michał Kalecki will be used according to our plans to remind Polish economic thought on the theory of economic cycles, economic growth models and economic problems of developing countries. Mr. Messengers! Gentlemen! Development cooperation is an expression of our solidarity with developing countries. Remembering the solidarity we experienced in difficult times, today we want to share this solidarity with others. It is also an investment in the stability and security of the world, through it and Poland. Development cooperation also involves fulfilling the obligations arising from Poland's membership of international organisations. We are committed to the efforts of the international community to implement the Sustainable Development Goals as part of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, to combat poverty, to social development and to help communities affected by armed conflicts. This Decision shall enter Poland has committed to official development aid according to preliminary estimates - estimates are still ongoing, but we can already say - 2800 million PLN. Our eastern neighbours, especially Belarus, Georgia, Moldova and Ukraine, remain a key direction of activity. In our opinion, the only way to stabilise the area of the former Soviet Union in the long term is through deep reforms and Polish modernisation. In these countries we are involved, among others. to support the construction of professional civil service, leading the Eastern Partnership Academy of Public Administration. We also support the development of a safe border management system. Five African countries remain an important geographical priority for Polish development cooperation: Ethiopia, Kenya, Senegal, Tanzania and Uganda, as well as three countries Asia and the Middle East: Myanmar, Lebanon and Palestine. Development aid includes projects developing human capital, entrepreneurship and environmental standards, as well as scholarships for studies in Poland. A good example of development activities is the project implemented for 4 years in Kenya by Polish firefighters and the Polish Centre for International Aid. It includes specialized training for fire services there, which increase their professionalism and effectiveness. Thanks to Polish aid in the suburbs of Nairobi, the first training center in Kenya and the Horn of Africa is created. 40% have already been trained Kenyan firefighters, about 450 people, who were equipped with modern rescue equipment at the same time. Humanitarian aid to victims of wars and conflicts is also an integral part of Polish foreign policy. We are active in Iraq, Lebanon, Jordan, Syria and Ukraine. Last year we took medical care and rehabilitation of 30,000. people, mostly women and children. We have provided first aid and shelter of over 35 thousand. refugees. We are in favour of a systemic vision of action that involves helping and responding effectively to the challenges closest to humanitarian and migration conflicts, rather than the unconditional automatic reception of all migrants. We are the biggest donor of the European Investment Bank's economic strengthening initiative, with the allocation of EUR 50 million, among others. to support the economic development of Lebanon and Jordan as countries affected by the refugee crisis. Together with the Visegrad Group countries, we also finance actions for better border management of Libya. We are consistently increasing funding for humanitarian aid. In 2015, it was PLN 24 million, while last year it was PLN 135 million. Our contributions support the priorities of the Polish development cooperation programme and, in the case of the UN contribution, also the priorities of the Polish Presidency of the Security Council. At the same time, we are strengthening relations with the most important partners of the global humanitarian aid system. In 2018 I signed an agreement on cooperation with the International Committee of the Red Cross on behalf of Poland. High Seym! The position of the country depends to a large extent on the assessments made by the world public on it. Good associations with Poland, knowledge and respect for its culture and history are crucial for the success of our projects in the international arena. The promotion of Polish culture and Poland abroad has a special role for Polish institutes, which operate in 25 countries. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs has signed an agreement with the Ministry of Culture and National Heritage, which aims to ensure better coordination of projects implemented by Polish institutes and other diplomatic and consulary institutions and to exploit the potential of all national cultural institutions and promote Polish culture abroad. In 2018, our diplomats in cooperation with the Adam Mickiewicz Institute and Polish representatives commemorated 100 years of independent Poland, carrying out over 1200 exhibitions, publications, conferences, films and other forms of promotion. The most famous moment of international celebrations was the highlight of the white-red tones of several dozen prestigious objects scattered on five continents, so we arrived with information about this unique anniversary to millions of customers around the world. We disseminate knowledge about Polish history and heroic attitudes of Poles. In Brussels, General Maczek's actions are reminiscent of the "Armoured wings" exhibition dedicated to the Polish liberators of Flanders. In January this year, I was honored to inaugurate this exhibition together with my Belgian counterpart. Thanks to the enormous work and efforts of the Embassy of the Republic of Poland in Bern, the whole world learned about members of the so-called. Berne's group, Polish diplomats in Switzerland under the direction of Alexander Ładoś, who during the Holocaust rescued Jews, giving them massively false passports of Latin American countries. As a result of the joint activities of the Ministry of Culture and National Heritage and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Krakow's raccoons were considered valuable enough to be included in the representative list of intangible cultural heritage of UNESCO humanity. This list is a list of practices and phenomena from around the world that show the diversity of intangible heritage... [[It's a success, actually...]] ...and raise awareness of its significance. Outside Poland, 20 million of our compatriots or people with Polish roots live today. Some of them are Poles remaining in the East after moving the borders of Poland, some are emigrants consisting of contemporary Polonia. Both of these groups, far from uniform, face different problems and require a different approach. In 2017, an amendment to the Repatriation Act adopted by the High House entered into force. On the basis of it, the Ministry continues its efforts to make the widest possible use of its instrumentary with the intention of increasing the repatriation scale of all Poles and Polish people willing to do so from the areas of the Asian part of the former Soviet Union, especially Kazakhstan. [[Applause]] In 2017, 298 visas were issued for repatriation, while in 2018 717. At a high level there is an interest in the Polish Charter. More than 22,000 were spent last year. Cards were extended to over 13,000. cases. We are consistently improving the functioning of consular offices. The Consular Information Centre and the Integrated Business Phone allow our compatriots to obtain help outside official working hours. Since the launch of both services on 30 January 2017, operators have already handled more than 170 thousand. telephone calls. The public's attention has recently drawn attention to the activities of our institution in Norway to protect the parental rights of Polish citizens and the rights of children themselves. Polish consuls contact Norwegian social services, verify their actions and intervene, if necessary, to protect the family life of our citizens. Protecting the rights of Poles is one of the foundations of our diplomacy. We also seek to improve bilateral cooperation with Norway in the protection of minors. At the beginning of March, a study visit took place a few days ago from the Norwegian Children's Protection Offices, during which we discussed issues that were the biggest challenge for cross-border cooperation. The increase in the emigration of Poles is of course not in the Polish national interest and its inspiration is not our intention, it is not the intention of the government. The economic growth of Poland, the improvement of the standard of living of its citizens and the decline of unemployment have a chance to induce many Poles to return to the country. We're counting on it, and we're talking about it. At the same time, the presence of such a large Polish community outside the country is an opportunity to actively promote Polish interests. It is particularly important to support Poland in further strengthening Poland's security, to realise the image of reforms implemented by our government and to promote the truth about Polish history. We would like our compatriots to become an important ally of the government in promoting the Polish state, Polish culture and national memory, the Polish historical narrative. We will gradually change these proportions, strengthen non-European institutions, because that is where the focus of the world economy is moving, and there we have more and more interests. Employees working at the MFA Centre are currently deployed in various, often remote locations in Warsaw, which makes coordination more difficult and cost-effective. So we decided to start building a building on our property, which adheres to the main building of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. This investment will allow the transfer of employees to one place, improve management, reduce the costs of running the resort by limiting the rental. Investments are also carried out abroad. Last year we put into use the combined headquarters of the embassy and Polish Institute in the renovated palace of Pacs in Vilnius. These events prompt us to sum up, but also to set new horizons for our European Community. It should be stressed that the balance of Polish membership in the European Union is certainly positive. Poland is becoming a competitive country thanks to investments co-financed by EU funds, open borders and the common market. Our wealth is constantly increasing. Art. 3 The Treaty on European Union states that the objective of the European Union is to ensure peace, its values and the prosperity of its peoples. And it's happening. Poland, thanks to the work of Poles, but also the common agricultural policy and cohesion policy, has increased its GDP by 23% per person since 2004. Today, thanks to good rule by law and justice, 5% economic growth and the sealing of the tax system, we can afford to reduce the income disparities of Poles, but also to the equation for the richer countries of the European Union. Social programs, including recently announced by President Jarosław Kaczyński, serve this purpose. It's a European programme. Thus, Poland makes good use of its participation in the European Community. The locals feel it, too. According to a Eurobarometer survey, 70% Poles believe that membership of the European Union is a good thing and 87% believes that European integration is beneficial for Poland. Our results are higher, much higher than the EU average - 62% respectively And 68%. A debate on its future is currently taking place in the European Union. Undoubtedly, the cause for deeper reflection is Brexit. In the European Parliament, individual Heads of Government present their visions for the development of the European Union. Our Polish vision was presented by Prime Minister Mateusz Morawiecki on 7 July 2018. The Prime Minister presented a diagnosis of existential crises faced by the European Union in recent years, but also a Polish vision of the future of integration, in which a renewal of the social contract guaranteeing European peoples a sense of security and hope for economic prosperity is called for. ParlaMint-PL_2019-03-14-sejm-78-2.u42	Dear Marshal! Dear Minister, Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, In my speech, I would like to focus on regional cooperation in our region of Europe. When we talk about the structures of such cooperation, the Visegrad Group comes to mind first of all. In recent years, however, new, very interesting and promising forms of cooperation have emerged, such as the Tri-Morga project, the Bucharest Nine and the so-called. the Berlin Process. Nor should we forget the Central European Initiative, which Poland has co-created since 1991. I would like to say a few words about each of these forms. The Visegrad Group is already a brand in itself today. The organization consisting of Poland, the Czech Republic, Slovakia and Hungary was established in 1991 and was modelled on existing forms of regional cooperation in Europe, for example. cooperation between Benelux States. It was primarily about agreeing positions and presenting them jointly to large partners. This increased the voice of individual countries and gave a negotiating advantage. In fact, this remains the main task of the group until today. The group's activity focuses on issues relating to the European Union agenda, including: cohesion policy, the common agricultural policy, the multiannual financial framework, the single market, the digital market, the neighbourhood policy, etc. Interesting opportunities include meetings in the V4 formula. For example, on 25 April in Bratislava there will be a meeting of premieres in V4 format plus Japan. In June, the Slovak Tatras are scheduled to meet the Prime Ministers of V4 with the Prime Minister of Austria. The Parliaments of the Visegrad Group also have something to show for it. Regular cooperation shall be carried out at the level of the Presidents of Parliament and of the elected parliamentary committees. And so on. At the last meeting in Bratislava on 26 February, representatives of the V4 Parliaments Committee adopted a declaration presenting a common view on a number of issues on the current political agenda. Among other things, it was presented in the magazine. solidarity with Ukraine, the Russian aggression was condemned and the Nord Stream 2 pipeline was jointly spoken against. Visegrad cooperation, however, is more than just political cooperation. Since 2000, the International Visegrad Fund has been operating. The grant and scholarship programmes implemented over the past years have helped develop networks between NGOs, academic and scientific environments, as well as local authorities in the region of Central and Eastern Europe. It should be stressed that such informal contacts and channels of cooperation provide great support for policy initiatives carried out within the Visegrad Group. Then the Tri-Morch initiative. This is a young but very promising form of economic and political cooperation. It was created only in 2015 and now brings together 12 countries of our region. After the initiative of Polish presidents Andrzej Duda and Croatia, Kolinda Grabar-Kitarović formed a Tri-Morga group, the initiative started the process of institutionalization. The support expressed by the US President at the second summit of the Warsaw Group in July 2017 for this initiative has made many eyes very attentive to the Tri-Morga. This can be proved by German declarations of willingness to develop cooperation with the group. Poland sees the Tri-More as a mechanism of cooperation for catching up on development delays resulting from the history of the region. This is not what needs to be pointed out, an initiative built in opposition to the European Union. In our view, the Tri-marine initiative has the opportunity to strengthen the unity of the Union, which is an additional source of development for the whole of the European Union, and at the same time to give impetus to the development of its neighbours. It is worth mentioning two large investments, which are, as it were, the flagship of the Tri-Moore, i.e. on the construction of the Via Carpatia route and the construction of the gas connection between LNG terminals in Świnoujście and the Croatian island of Krk. Initiated in 2014 so-called The Berlin process includes 13 countries, 7 of which are EU countries and the rest are Western Balkan countries interested in membership of the European Union. Like the Tri-Morch initiative, the Berlin Process primarily sets itself economic goals. It is about the recovery of Balkan economies through the expansion of transport, energy and telecommunications infrastructure in the region. The countries involved in the project are also helping to implement Union law into the legal systems of the Balkan countries. The Eastern Partnership maintains the interest of the Union in countries located beyond its eastern borders, and the Berlin Process aims to draw the attention of the EU countries and institutions to the waiting in line for the membership of the Western Balkans. We support this process. Poland remains a constant supporter of the open door policy for the European Union. The enlargement process is the best guarantee of security, political stability, socio-economic development and good neighbourly relations in the Western Balkans, and the security and development of not only the region but also the whole of Europe depend on it. Political and economic cooperation has already been mentioned and it is worth mentioning the regional security initiative. I'm talking about the so-called. Bucharest's 9th, or so-called. format D9. This is an initiative on cooperation between nine Eastern countries of NATO. Like the Tri-Moor, it is not intended to break up the European Union, so Bucharest cooperation is not intended to replace NATO, which remains the primary pillar of our security policy. The aim of Bucharest cooperation is to complement the Alliance's activities and to enable common positions to be reached on matters resolved within the Alliance. It is about reaching NATO's allies with a common point of view on the threat to the eastern NATO flank and action to strengthen our eastern borders. Finally, it is worth mentioning the organisation of the Central European Initiative, the so-called "Central European Initiative." ISE. This is one of the oldest forms of regional cooperation in Central and Eastern Europe and, although it does not play a leading role, it remains a link between 18 countries from the Baltic to the Adriatic and the Black Sea. The Central European Initiative is increasingly focusing on project activities, moving away from political issues. Under the auspices of the ISE, the ISE Scientific and Technological Cooperation Network was established. They also work here so-called. ISE universities networks. Ladies and gentlemen! In conclusion, a few words of summary. Regional cooperation in Central Europe is extremely important for our country. It is true that security and economic links lead us to the west of Europe. The European Union and NATO remain priorities, and that will not change. However, close and strong regional cooperation with the countries of our part of Europe is an invaluable added value for our country. It increases our safety and gives us the chance to get together with our partners so needed we in a globalised world competitive advantage. Thank you very much. [[Applause]]

During World War II, Poland suffered the greatest losses of all European countries, both personal and material, in relation to the total population and national assets. These losses resulted not only from war activities, but mainly from German occupation policy, in particular from the deliberate and organized extermination of the population on occupied Polish lands, intensive exploitation of Polish society, including forced labour, intentional destruction of property, and in particular the destruction of virtually 90% of the capital of the state, Warsaw, as well as thousands of Polish cities, towns and villages. The negative effects of World War II are still felt in terms of demography, economy, infrastructure, science, education and culture. Every Pole and every Polish woman still suffer from the negative effects of World War II. Each year of war and occupation carried the Polish state to a lower level of development in all aspects of public, economic or social life. For generations, Poles lost their ability to accumulate capital, savings, and works of art and cultural goods, valuable possessions and achievements of their ancestors were stolen and exported by the Germans. The modern Polish state today would be in a completely different place on the map of the world and Europe, if there were no World War II and its tragic consequences. Several decimated generations of Poles were sentenced to an enormous effort to rebuild war damage and to lift the country from ruins. To this day, the Republic of Poland has received neither the full amount of war reparations granted to the great powers in Potsdam by decision, nor even more compensation appropriate to the damage suffered by war and occupation. Also to this day, Germany has failed to account for the activities of robberies and robberies carried out systematically in relation to the works of culture and art of the Polish state and its citizens. Poland and Poles did not recover the stolen hundreds of thousands of cultural goods, works of art seized during World War II. Moreover, Germany questions political and legal responsibility towards Poland for the effects of World War II, and their actions are unfortunately limited only to symbolic gestures and words about moral responsibility. Despite this possibility, Germany has avoided and avoided the regulation of the case in the form of bilateral agreements. Protecting their interests and economic resources has always been more important to them. Since the end of World War II, German policy on this issue can be characterized as a game of silence, statute of limitations and oblivion.
